- [x] **A refused router leaves no entry (decided and built Nov 17, 2026).** Closes "the router guard leaves a refused router with zero counts each step". Before, a refused router kept its slot, so every record carried `moe`, `moe_top_k` and `moe_stats` for it: eight zeros, `routed_tokens: 0`, `diversity: 0.0`. Both detectors already read a zero-sum router as a `missing` sample, and `input_counts` counted no MoE record for it. So no false incident and no history came of it. But the zeros read as data in the records, the MCP series and the dashboards. A run whose only router was refused also read `not_configured`, which is not the same as "asked for and could not be judged".
  - *What was built.* `_decode` in `capture.py` leaves a refused router out of all three keys. A record whose routers were all refused has no MoE keys. The refusal sticks for the session: the guard reads shapes, so a short batch (8 counts on 4 tokens) would pass a step that full size refuses, and that step's counts would be the same misread. A router with holes in its series would be worse than none. The one-time warning is unchanged. The first record written after the refusal carries `moe_refused: {router: reason}`, once per router per session. Records are what the evidence and the server read; the run manifest is written at `init`, before any forward pass. The key travels in `extra_json`, so the Flight schema is unchanged. `refused_routers_not_checked` in `evidence.py`, and the same entry in `detect.rs`, add a `not_checked` entry (`check: "router"`, the routers, the reason, the step). `kinds: [moe_imbalance, starved_experts]` makes those kinds `unavailable`, as a failed probe does for attention. A model with no refused router writes byte-identical records (a two-router run, routers-only and full capture, compared before and after with `attempt` and `ts` removed). The golden files are unchanged and were not regenerated.
  - *Taken from the reference repos.* moewatch's watcher (`_watcher.py`) analyses nothing for a step with no layer stats and writes no zeros for it. TrainCheck's checker (`checker.py`) counts and logs the invariants that were "not triggered" apart from the ones that passed. That is the line drawn here between `unavailable` and `checked`. Megatron's MoE tracker, W&B and MLflow were not read for this.
  - *What a reader now sees.* With a refused router, the MoE kinds read `unavailable` even when a router beside it was counted. This follows the failed-probe precedent. The run is then `degraded` (reason `not_checked`). The counted router's records still count as `records` toward history. A refused router no longer takes a `layer` number, so the routers after it are numbered one lower than before.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_router_guard.py`, now 8 tests:
    - Only router refused: no MoE keys, `moe_refused` once, no samples, `input_counts["moe"] == 0`, both kinds `unavailable`.
    - A refused router beside a counted one, over two blocks: the counted one at 240 tokens on every step, history and samples its own, `layer` 1.
    - Under `torch.compile`, and a model with no refusal: nothing written.
    - Streamed to the real server with one router refused, then with all refused: stored equals written, and the Rust path's `not_checked`, `kind_checks` and samples equal Python's.
  - *Rust.* One unit test, `a_refused_router_makes_the_moe_kinds_unavailable_and_says_why`.
  - *Run, macOS.* Rust: 85 unit and 31 integration tests, clippy `--no-default-features`, fmt. Python: 1,321 passed and 15 skipped in one full run with `PYTHONPATH=src`, which the tests that start `python -m tensorquill` or a rank as a subprocess need when the package is not installed. Every skip is an optional package that is not installed (`accelerate`, `boto3`, `rerun`); none is a golden, kind-checks parity or server-detect test. The command line was run on a run with a refused router: a local file and a server store, with the Python and the Rust detectors and with `--rank all`. Each printed the MoE kinds `unavailable` and the run `degraded (not checked: router)`. The all-ranks summary names the check but, like any per-rank `not_checked` entry, does not print its reason. The experiment tests were not run: they need `rerun`, which is not installed here.
  - *Open.*
    - Steps already written before the refusal are not withdrawn. The refusal applies to every block queued after the hook found it, including steps of that block that ran before it.
    - A router given an `indices=` function is still not checked. *Closed Nov 17, 2026: see "An `indices=` router is checked, and the reason names the fixes".*
    - nanoMoE itself was not run.
    - CI on Linux was not run.
    - `compare` (`divergence.py`) lists keys it does not know under `not_compared`, so `moe_refused` will show there. Left to the sessions on `compare.py`; there is a note in the shared folder.
- [x] **An `indices=` router is checked, and the reason names the fixes (decided and built Nov 17, 2026).** Closes "a router given an `indices=` function is still not checked".
  - *The user's decision.* One refused router keeps the run `degraded` and `moe_imbalance` and `starved_experts` `unavailable`, even with a counted router beside it. The user asked for that router, and a collapse there would go unseen. What changed is the reason, which must name the router and the two fixes. Before, the warning named the fixes but the `not_checked` reason read only "router NAME is not counted: WHY". For a router that already had `indices=`, "pass `indices=`" was no fix.
  - *What is checked, by how the router is named.*
    - *Default.* An integer tensor in the output is refused when its size is not a whole multiple of the tokens (as before); its values are not checked. Without one, the top-`top_k` of the first float tensor whose last dimension is `n_experts` is used; those indices are in range by construction.
    - *`indices=`, new.* The function is refused if it raises (caught, never raised into training, under `torch.compile` too), or returns a non-tensor, a float, bool or complex tensor, a tensor on another device than the counts (which used to raise into training), the wrong size for the tokens, or any value outside `0..n_experts-1` on a step.
    - *A named inner module.* It is read as the default is.
    - *Still not checked.* Logits whose last dimension is not `n_experts` (that router records zero counts on every step and is not refused), whether a multiple of the tokens matches `top_k`, and the size check when the router's first argument has fewer than two dimensions.
  - *No new sync.* The value-range check is device work only. An `indices=` router's counts buffer has one extra slot, which adds the indices that match no expert (`numel - hits.sum()`). `flush` reads that slot from the counts it already copies to the CPU, and `_decode` drops it. Default routers keep the buffer and the device work they had; `_count_width` gives the width in all three places. Records are unchanged, so a router counted in earlier blocks is refused from the block in which a value left the range. That state is new, and it is tested in Python and through the real server. The warning is also said from `flush`, so a refusal found at `close` is not left unsaid. Not checked on a GPU that no sync was added; on CPU, three ways of naming a router (default, `indices=`, an inner module) wrote byte-identical records before and after, with `attempt`, `ts`, `system` and the timing keys removed.
  - *One text, built once in the capture.* `_refusal` in `capture.py` writes "WHY. To count it, FIX", with FIX chosen by case and filled with the router's name, `n_experts` and `top_k`. It is the warning ("TensorQuill is not counting the router 'NAME': TEXT. Its expert loads are not recorded.") and the value of `moe_refused`. `refused_routers_not_checked` and `detect.rs` keep their wrapper "router NAME is not counted: TEXT" unchanged, so Python and Rust cannot drift and older records read as before. It holds no "; ", the join between routers. What each place printed on a refused-router run stored by the real server:
    - *Evidence file*, `not_checked[].reason` and `kind_checks.{moe_imbalance,starved_experts}.reason` read "router refused is not counted: its output's first integer tensor has shape (8,), which is not expert indices for the 120 tokens it was called on. To count it, pass routers={'refused': tensorquill.Router(8, 2, indices=...)} with a function that returns the chosen expert indices from its output, or name the module inside it whose output is the router's logits". The Rust report gives the same.
    - *Text summary* prints "Not checked (router): " and that reason, then "Run state: degraded (not checked: router)".
    - *`--rank all`* keeps the reason in each rank's `not_checked`; its summary prints only the counts line and "degraded (not checked: router)", as before.
    - *Compact evidence* copies it unchanged. *MCP* `list_incidents` and `get_all_ranks_status` return it in `not_checked`, with `ranks`.
    - *`compare`* prints only "unavailable in B" for the two kinds; the reason is not in its output or file. Not changed here.
    - For an `indices=` router the fix reads "To count it, change its indices= function to return the chosen expert indices, integers from 0 to 7, one or 2 for each token, or drop indices= and name the module inside it whose output is the router's logits".
  - *Taken from the reference repos.* moewatch's `RouterHook` wraps extraction in `try/except`, warns a few times and never raises into the forward pass; that is taken. It reads indices with `.cpu()`/`.item()` (a sync) and takes `n_experts` from the largest index, which hides out-of-range values. Neither is taken: here the range is judged on the device and refused.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_router_guard.py`, now 18: wrong shape; a float, bool or list result, or one on another device; a function that raises (eager and compiled); values out of range in the second block (counted, then refused, samples `missing` after); the exact reason in the warning, the record, `not_checked`, `kind_checks` and the summary; and through the real server, the default reason's text and the counted-then-refused router, with the Rust `not_checked`, `kind_checks` and samples equal to Python's.
  - *Run, macOS.* Rust: 85 unit and 31 integration tests, fmt, clippy with and without default features. Python, with the worktree's server binary and no skips: 1,362 passed in the final full run. A first full run, before the extra slot was limited to `indices=` routers and the device test was added, had 1,360 passed and 1 failed: `test_all_ranks_rust.py::test_a_change_of_status_alone_is_written`. Its traceback was not kept, so the cause is not known. Nothing in this change touches it, and it passed in three runs of its file alone and in the final run. With `pyarrow` made unimportable, the router-guard file skips its three server tests and passes the rest. Experiments: 81 passed. The Gate 1 and bake-off fixtures and the golden files are unchanged: `git status` shows no change under `experiments` or the goldens after the runs, and their tests pass.

    - Dropped tokens. Naming `router.w_g` (its logits, before the drop) still counts it. Whether loads should be counted before or after the capacity drop is not decided.

    - Output on another device than the counts (default router, inner module): raised. Now "its output's first integer tensor (or floating-point tensor) is on X, not on Y where the counts are". The fix named is the one that holds: an `indices=` function that returns the indices on the counts' device. The inner module is not offered, since its logits would be on that device too. An `indices=` router on another device was refused already; its text is unchanged.
    - Logits whose last dimension is not `n_experts`, or a 0-dimensional first floating-point tensor, with no integer tensor (a block that returns its hidden states first): zeros on every step.     - uint16/32/64 indices (default or `indices=`): `==` with int64 raised. float8 logits: no `argmax` or `topk`, raised. A sparse tensor: `reshape` raised. A DTensor (tensor parallelism): mixing it with a plain tensor raised. All four are judged from type, layout and dtype and refused.

Under `torch.compile` there is no `try` in the hook, since an op that fails fails the trace first; the checks above are what keep it from raising there, and a type they do not foresee could still raise under compile.
    - Warnings turned into errors (`-W error`): the refusal's `RuntimeWarning` raises out of the next `optimizer.step()`. That was so for every refusal before; it is new for wrong-width logits, which trained on with zeros. Not changed.
    - `torch.export` of a hooked model, strict and not: nothing raised and nothing refused, before or now.
    - No tensor in the output (`None`, `()`), a module called with keyword arguments only, a module called under `torch.inference_mode()`: none raised, before or now. - [x] **Evaluation forwards are left out of the records (found Nov 17, 2026 by the papers review; built the same day in a side session).** The capture hooks fired on every forward and their buffers were emptied only when an optimizer step was committed, so an evaluation between two steps went into the next step's record. `session.phase("eval")` only told the heartbeat.
  - *Reproduced, CPU, eager and compiled.* Three evaluation batches of 10 tokens between steps 1 and 2 made step 2's router counts `[0, 9, 15, 16]` (40 tokens) against `[0, 3, 3, 4]` on the others, and its `abs_max` that of the evaluation's inputs (207 against 2.18). The same with the phase set, without it, and under `torch.inference_mode()`. Inflated or skewed loads on the step after each evaluation can raise or hide `moe_imbalance` and `starved_experts`.
  - *What leaked, statistic by statistic.*
    - Router counts (`moe`, `moe_stats`, the evidence's samples): leaked, summed in.
    - Layer `abs_max`: leaked (a maximum over the step's forwards). Layer `mean`, `std`, `zero_frac` and the seen flag: leaked when the evaluation came after the step's last training forward (between `backward` and `optimizer.step`), since they are the last forward's, and a layer that runs only in evaluation got an entry of its own.
    - Forwards before the first step (Lightning's validation sanity check): went into step 0.
    - The probe: no leak. It runs in eval mode without gradients and the session zeroed the buffers after it; it now also passes the new rule. Its own hooks run only while it is active, and the attention statistics come from it alone.
    - Gradient norms, clipping, weights, the loss logged through Accelerate's `backward`: no leak. An evaluation has no backward and no optimizer step.
    - System readings: not this leak. The allocator's peak is the process's, read once a flush and never reset, so an evaluation's memory is in it by design.
    - Timestamps: an evaluation's time sits between two steps' `ts`, which is what the heartbeat's phase explains. Not changed.
    - `data_wait`: already counted only in the `train` phase, so without `session.phase("eval")` a hand-written loop's evaluation loader wait is still added to the next step. `session.data` and `watch_loader` on an evaluation loader add its batches to the next step's data record. Neither is a forward and the phase is the only signal there. Reported, not changed.
    - Adapters: the Lightning callback (validation and its sanity check), the Hugging Face callback (`eval_strategy="steps"`) and an Accelerate loop that evaluates with `no_grad` all leaked through the hooks, as any loop does. Checked through each.
  - *The rule (`capture._evaluating`).* A forward is left out when the hooked module is in eval mode and gradients are off; under `session.phase("eval")`, when either is. The two signals together are how Lightning (`model.eval()` and `no_grad` or `inference_mode` around its loop), the Hugging Face `Trainer` (`model.eval()` and `no_grad` in `prediction_step`) and most hand-written loops evaluate, so it works without the phase. Neither alone is safe: reentrant activation checkpointing runs its training forward with gradients off (checked: `torch.is_grad_enabled()` is False there in train mode; the Hugging Face default is non-reentrant), and some training keeps modules in eval mode (frozen blocks, dropout off). The phase alone never drops a forward: a phase left behind by a callback that missed its end would drop a training step.
  - *Taken from the reference repos.* SageMaker Debugger has TRAIN, EVAL and PREDICT modes, each with its own step count and save settings, but the user sets them (`hook.set_mode`); the PyTorch hook does not read `module.training`. That is what `session.phase` is here, and its separate evaluation records are not taken: for this change evaluation is dropped, with no new record type or incident kind. `wandb.watch` counts forwards with no notion of mode, so it has the same leak in its histograms. Lightning's evaluation loop sets eval mode and `no_grad`/`inference_mode`, which is the default signal taken.
  - *Unchanged for training.* A training-only run writes byte-identical records, with `attempt`, `ts`, `system` and the timing keys removed: the ten router configurations of "A router the counting cannot read …" (each eager and compiled) and seven more (gradient accumulation of three, reentrant and non-reentrant checkpointing in eager mode, a hooked block kept in eval mode with `capture="full"`, `capture="full"`, a probe every two steps, and a forward without gradients in train mode before each step, which is still counted), against `ff969f7`. No device work is added, so no sync.
  - *Dynamo counts.* Each value a hook reads becomes a guard; Dynamo always guards on grad mode but on a module's `training` only when something reads it. So with gradients on the hook reads the phase flag first and `training` only under the eval phase, and with gradients off `training` first. The training graph gains one guard, on the flag (a bool set on its own object from the start: a first version that kept it on the class compiled the training graph a third time at the first `set_phase`). Train, evaluate, train, evaluate, train, `torch.compile(backend="eager")`, unique graphs without TensorQuill and with it (two routers, `capture="full"`), no graph break in any:

    | Evaluation | No Dropout: unhooked | hooked | hooked, `ff969f7` | With Dropout: unhooked | hooked |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | eval mode, `no_grad` | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
    | eval mode, `inference_mode` | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
    | phase, eval mode, `no_grad` | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
    | phase, eval mode, gradients on | 1 | **2** | 1 | 2 | 2 |
    | phase, train mode, `no_grad` | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
    | eval mode, gradients on, no phase | 1 | 1 | 1 | not run | not run |
    | train mode, `no_grad`, no phase | 2 | 2 | 2 | not run | not run |

    The one extra graph is the cost of the phase covering an evaluation with gradients on, in a model where nothing else depends on the mode: that evaluation must compile without the hooks' work. A first version, which read `training` on every forward, added a graph whenever `model.eval()` was called with gradients on; the measurement caught it. Only the `eager` backend was run here.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_eval_forwards.py`, 24, each comparing a run with an evaluation to the same run without it: the reproduction three ways (eager and compiled); an evaluation inside a step and before the first step (eager and compiled; covers the mean, std, zero fraction and a layer seen only in evaluation); gradient accumulation still summed; one signal enough under the phase; one signal alone without the phase still counted (phase alone, eval mode alone, `no_grad` alone); a hooked module kept in eval mode during training still recorded; an evaluation batch cannot refuse a router; Lightning validation with its sanity check; the Hugging Face `Trainer` with `eval_strategy="steps"`; an Accelerate loop. Against `ff969f7`, 20 of them fail; the four that pass are the edges that are meant to be unchanged.
  - *Run, macOS.* Python with the worktree's release server and pyarrow, no skips: 1,446 passed (run again after the guard order was changed; the same count before). The router-guard and evaluation files with `pyarrow` made unimportable: 79 passed, the 4 server tests skipped. Experiments: 92 passed. No Rust change, so `cargo test` was not run again. `git status` shows no change under `experiments` or the golden files after the runs.
  - *Open.*
    - Evaluation statistics are not recorded: left out, not kept apart. A record of their own, or an incident kind for them, is not built.
    - An evaluation in eval mode with gradients on (no `no_grad`), or in train mode without gradients, outside `session.phase("eval")`, still reaches the next step. So does `session.data` on an evaluation loader, and `data_wait` without the phase.
    - A hooked module in eval mode inside reentrant activation checkpointing is left out of training (both signals off).
    - A `session.phase("eval")` left set (a callback that missed its end) drops a hooked module kept in eval mode on the next training forward with gradients on, until `_commit` clears the phase; a router in that state writes zeros for that step.
    - Found on the way, not changed: activation checkpointing of a block holding a hooked module, inside `torch.compile(backend="eager")`, raises into training ("one of the variables needed for gradient computation has been modified by an inplace operation", the counts buffer). Seen on main (`3e63842`) and on this branch, reentrant and non-reentrant, with routers and with `capture="full"`; not with `aot_eager` (the path Inductor takes) and not without TensorQuill. Inductor itself was not run. Only eager-mode checkpointing is tested (`test_review_fixes.py`).
    - No GPU run, no Linux run.

    | Store | Exact parsing on | Off | Nov 16, 2026 |

The code merged without a conflict; only this file conflicted, where each branch had added its entry at the same place, and all three entries are kept.
  - *The two rules that were built apart are tested together.* The startup bound was cut from main, so it never ran with "a stall at critical fires". `test_a_real_rank_that_never_takes_a_step_starts_the_command_once_when_it_is_critical` in `sdk/tests/test_starting_bound.py` runs a real server (start limit 3 s, critical 4 s later), a real process that never steps, and `python -m tensorquill <run folder> --watch --rank all --on-incident COMMAND` with no `--fire-kind`. Nothing starts at `warning`; the command starts once at `critical`, for `job_stalled` at step 0. With the firing rule broken on purpose (`STALL_FIRES_AT` set to another word) the test fails. It uses the all-ranks view because a run that never stored a batch has a status and no part; the one-rank watcher on such a folder was not tried.

During the start limit (1800 s by default) the server cannot yet call a rank that has shown no step a stall, so for that stretch the two kinds say more than is true. The wait is in time, not records, and is the server's.

The test's helper now names the binary the file was collected with when the environment names none; the file's 24 tests then pass with the variable unset. - [x] **Enough history (built Nov 16, 2026 in a side session; merged Nov 17, 2026).** Item 2 of "Four open choices settled": a kind stays `checked` from its first record, a rule that is warming up does not degrade the run, and each kind now says whether its rule has had enough history. Too few probes is no longer a `not_checked` entry.
  - *The fields.* In `kind_checks`, in `evidence.json` and in the server's report, each of the 25 kinds read from step records gains `records_needed` beside `records`, and `enough_history`, which is `records >= records_needed` whatever the value. The other seven kinds have no count: their source is whole once it is read, so `enough_history` is true exactly when the kind is `checked`.
  - *The definition of "needed", one for every kind.* For each rule of the kind, take the fewest records it can report an incident on. The kind needs the largest of those, so from then on every one of its rules could have fired. A kind with one rule and no warm-up needs 1. A reader can check a number by hand against the rule's constants; `RECORDS_NEEDED` in `evidence.py` lists each kind's rules beside the table, and `detect.rs` has the same table.
  - *The numbers.*
    - `moe_imbalance` 11: the diversity drop of a top-k router (k of 3 or more) needs 10 earlier samples and the one judged. The share and collapse rules need 1.
    - `starved_experts` 50: a full window, and it is the `starve_window` the run was judged with, not the default.
    - `grad_spike` 13: one parameter needs 10 earlier norms and then 3 in a row. The total needs 11.
    - `stalled_weights` 3. `loss_scale_trouble` 10 (skips in a row; a scale below 1 needs 1). `data_stalled` 4 (the same batch: the batch and 3 repeats; no batch needs 3). `epoch_order_repeated` 4 (a pass of two different batches, and the next pass matching both).
    - `activation_spike` 11. `activation_drift` and `dead_activations` 60 (the first 50 records as the baseline and 10 more).
    - The three attention kinds 4: two probes as the baseline, then two in a row.
    - `memory_growth` 6. `hardware_errors` 2 (an ECC count above the attempt's first reading; an Xid needs 1). `input_starvation` 21 (the rise: the first full window as the baseline and one more step; the share needs 20).
    - 1 for the other nine: `non_finite`, `replica_drift`, `memory_pressure`, `allocator_retries` and `gpu_throttled` read one record, and `repeated_steps` and the three resume kinds compare with what earlier attempts wrote, which the count of records leaves out once a later attempt has replaced them.
  - *Why the slowest rule and not the fastest.* The first design took the fewest records on which a kind can report at all. It gave `moe_imbalance` 1 though the rule that judges a Granite router needs 11, and `loss_scale_trouble` 1 though the run of skips needs 10. A false "not yet" costs nothing; a false "enough" is what this item exists to stop. The price: a kind can report through a faster rule before it has enough history. The summary and the listing then mark it ("but reported by a faster rule", `reported`), and the incident stands. Five golden files show this (four Gate 1 fixtures of 4 to 9 records, and `edge low scale`).
  - *Confirmed by the author the same day: the slowest rule stays.* "Enough history" has to mean every rule of the kind could have fired, or the field gives the false all-clear it was built to remove. An incident is positive evidence and stands on its own, so a redundant caveat beside it is much cheaper than a wrong all-clear.
  - *Entered in the benchmark's amendments.* The five MoE fixtures of Gate 1 are 4 to 31 records long, so their summaries now carry the clause, and four show `moe_imbalance` as reported by a faster rule. `experiments/gate1/PREREGISTRATION.md` has it under "Amendments", with each fixture's line. No question, key or score changes.
  - *It is a floor, said as one.* `records` counts every record that carried the input, and a rule may count fewer: a parameter, module or router seen on fewer records has less history of its own; a total that is not finite or belongs to a skipped step is left out; the memory, hardware and data-wait rules start over at each attempt; an epoch must end before the next can be compared with it; a starved expert also needs enough tokens in the window. So false means the kind's slowest rule cannot have fired, and true means the count has reached what that rule needs. The attention kinds used the most probes any one module was seen on; they now use the count of probes that recorded attention, which differs only when probes do not name the same modules.
  - *Too few probes.* The `attention` entry of `not_checked` is no longer written, in Python or in Rust. A capture with probes but fewer than four reads `healthy`, with the three kinds `checked` and `enough_history` false. A probe that failed is still the `probe` entry, the kinds are `unavailable` and the run is `degraded`. The server's `watchdog.json` follows without a change to `watchdog.rs`: its run state degrades on the report's `not_checked`, and the entry is gone at its source.
  - *The 4,000th step, checked.* The capture probes on every `probe_every`-th step it commits, counting from 1 (`_cadence` in `capture.py`). A module's first two probes fill its baseline, the third is the first one judged and the fourth confirms it. So with `probe_every` 1,000 nothing can be reported before the 4,000th step of a run's first attempt, and the incident is then dated at the third probe, the 3,000th. A test runs a real capture of the toy model with `probe_every` 3 and finds probes on the 3rd, 6th, 9th and 12th steps, no attention incident possible at 11 steps and one at 12. Read and not run: after a resume the new session counts its committed steps from zero again, so the probes fall on its own 1,000th, 2,000th steps, while the rules keep the history of the probes that still count.
  - *Where a reader sees it.*
    - The summary: `6 of 32 kinds checked, 1 of them without enough history yet (grad_spike 3 of 13 records), 26 not configured`. The clause is there only when a checked kind is warming up.
    - `list_incidents` of the MCP server: `kinds.checked` holds only the kinds with enough history, and `kinds.not_enough_history` the rest, each with `records` and `records_needed`. The tool's description says "nothing found" is no verdict for those.
    - `sdk/AGENT_INSTRUCTIONS.md` has "checked, not enough history yet" as its own case, and says `healthy` does not mean every checked kind had enough history.

    1. Only judged ranks count. A rank whose report is not current is in no value and no list, and the count line names it as not counted.
    2. A kind read from one rank's step records (the server's 25) takes its worst judged rank's value: `unavailable` if any judged rank is, else `not_configured` if any is, else `checked`. So `checked` means every judged rank checked it, and "nothing found" for it is said of every judged rank.
    3. When the judged ranks differ, the entry lists them by value under `ranks`. A kind that reads `not_configured` or `unavailable` for the run can so be checked, and even reported, on the ranks named there. The count line marks it: "1 of them checked on some ranks only (stalled_weights on rank 0)", with "without enough history yet" when those ranks are still warming up.
    4. History the same way. `enough_history` is false when any judged rank is short, with the ranks under `not_enough_history_ranks`; it is left out when none is short and some rank's report does not say, which `kind_history` reads as `not_stated` and never as enough. `records` is the fewest any judged rank has and `records_needed` the most any needs.
    5. `job_stalled`, `rank_silent` and the three file kinds belong to the run: `other_kind_checks` of the run's status, which is expected because the source is a store, and of the files given.
    6. The two cross-rank kinds follow rule 2 over what each judged rank brings to the comparison, and need two ranks. `memory_imbalance` is `checked` when every judged rank's report has a peak memory reading and there are at least two. `duplicate_batches` is `checked` when every judged rank recorded `data`, there are at least two, and a status was read; without the status it is `unavailable`.

With no judged rank, or with a judged rank whose report has no usable `kind_checks` (a server older than the key wrote it), there is no run-level object and no count. `kind_checks_not_stated` gives the reason and the ranks, and the summary says "What was checked is not stated". A count over only the ranks that do say would read as the run's.
    8. `unavailable` on one rank makes the kind `unavailable` for the run, and the run is `degraded`. `run_state` was not changed: a judged rank's `not_checked` entry already degraded the all-ranks state, and a missing status already gave `no_status`. The two now say the same thing.

- [x] **A stall at critical fires the trigger, and one line says when the thresholds are not the defaults (built Nov 16, 2026 in a side session; merged Nov 17, 2026).** Items 4 and 1 of "Four open choices settled". Python and documentation only: `sdk/src/tensorquill/trigger.py`, three small functions and two calls in `evidence.py`, the README and the agent instructions. No Rust change was needed, and none was made.
  - *The firing rule.* A `job_stalled` or `rank_silent` incident whose `severity` is `critical` fires with no option. One at `warning` fires only when named with `--fire-kind`, as before. The rule is in `Trigger._fires`, which now reads the incident and not only its kind. `INCIDENT_CLASSES`, the run state and the golden files are untouched: the two kinds stay observations, and the command and the webhook get `observation` as the class.
  - *Once.* The key is still rank, kind and step. A stall named with `--fire-kind` fires at `warning` and not again at `critical`. One not named fires once, at `critical`. A restarted watcher reads `fired.json` and fires neither again. Retries and the state file are the same code as before. *(Changed Nov 17, 2026: a stall is keyed by its run; see Decisions: "The trigger and the watchdog: one firing for one stall ...".)*
  - *A declared pause cannot fire.* The server gives a severity only to a rank that is silent, unresponsive or lost (`run_status` in `watchdog.rs`, whose own test holds a paused rank's severity null), and an incident's severity is taken from its stopped ranks alone, in Rust (`progress_incidents`) and in Python (`_progress_incidents`). A test hands the Python reader a status that calls a paused rank critical, which the server never writes, and the incident stays a warning. So nothing was added to the trigger for it.
  - *How `critical` reaches the watcher of a job that sends nothing.* It was not known whether the server rewrites `watchdog.json` while no rank sends. It does. The server looks at each run every `--watchdog-seconds` on a timer of its own (`spawn_watchdog` in `ingest.rs`, which does not wait for anything to arrive), and writes the file when the status changed, each rank's severity being part of what it compares (`write_statuses` in `ingest.rs`), and at least every third look. The watcher reads the status on every look and writes the evidence again when a rank's severity changed (`_status_stamp`), then hands it to the trigger. With the defaults that is a 10 s look and a 2 s poll after the rank passes 900 s.
  - *Proved with a real server, twice.* One test starts `tensorquill-server` with `--silent-seconds 2 --critical-seconds 10`, a real training process that takes 300 steps and then stops stepping while its process, its stream and its heartbeats live, and two watchers through the command line with `--on-incident` and no `--fire-kind`: one rank's, and `--rank all`. While both evidence files list the stall at `warning`, nothing has started and `fired.json` is empty. The status is seen written three more times while no step arrives (the rank's heartbeats do, every 0.5 s). Each watcher's command then starts once, with `severity: critical`, `suspected_hang`, and `stuck_at` naming the function the rank is stuck in. More rewrites of the status and a restart of each watcher start nothing more. A second test holds two streams open on which nothing arrives at all, not a step and not a heartbeat (senders inside the test, `--silent-seconds 1 --critical-seconds 4`): the server's timer alone takes the stall from `warning` to `critical`, the all-ranks watcher writes each, and its command starts once, after the look that wrote `critical`.
  - *The thresholds line.* `evidence.thresholds_line(evidence)` returns one line that names each applied threshold that is not the default, with its value and the default, or None when all five are the defaults: `Thresholds that are not the defaults: the server applied overload_threshold 0.5 (default: 0.35), top_k 2 (default: what the run recorded).` `incident_summary` ends with it, for one rank's evidence and for the all-ranks view, so the command line prints it for every look it reports. It reads what the evidence already lists as applied: `detection.thresholds`, and `thresholds` or every set of `thresholds_by_rank` with its ranks. When the detectors ran here it says so in place of "the server applied". Values are compared as the reader compares them (`_same_threshold`), so a default that came back from JSON one unit in the last place apart prints nothing.
  - *At the defaults nothing changed.* No golden file holds summary text, and the Gate 1 test compares `summary.txt` with `incident_summary` computed in the same run. A test holds the summary at the defaults equal, line for line, to the summary with one threshold changed less its last line.
  - *Read plainly, open to narrowing.*
    1. Any stall at `critical` fires, whatever its `assessment`. A job that was killed without closing its streams is `lost_connection`, critical 900 s later, and fires.
    2. A stall whose kind or step changes is another incident and fires again, both for one hang: a silent rank at `critical` whose job then stops altogether (`rank_silent`, then `job_stalled`, already critical on its first look), and a second rank that falls silent at an earlier step, which moves the step of `rank_silent`. A test pins both. The earlier hand-over excused the moving step because the kind fired only by name; that no longer holds.
    3. Ranks that wait in `train` while another rank runs a long declared evaluation are silent, not paused, and fire at `critical`. The rank in the pause does not. Read in `watchdog.rs`, pinned in Python with a made status, not run with real ranks.
    4. A pause the server stops believing (no sign of work for `--pause-seconds`, 1800 by default, or a work counter that stopped) makes the rank silent with its quiet time counted from its last training step, so it is `critical` at once, with no warning first. Read in `watchdog.rs`, not run.
    5. A `watchdog.json` left at `critical` by a server that was killed fires once for a watcher started later. The evidence calls the status old; the incident is listed all the same. A test pins it.
    6. The line is printed only for evidence from a server's store, which is short of "whenever": a non-default option typed for a local `.jsonl` file, or for a read of a store without `--watch`, prints no line. The reader gave that option itself, so nothing is silent there. That evidence has no `detection.thresholds`; it holds three of the five as keys of their own (`overload_threshold`, `starve_window`, `starve_ratio`), `top_k` only as what each router resolved to, and no `drift_tolerance`, so a line from it would be silent on two. Naming all five there needs a `thresholds` key in `build_evidence`, which the golden files freeze.
    7. In the all-ranks view with `thresholds_by_rank`, a set at the defaults is not named in the line. The block above it still lists every set.
  - *Found on the way, not changed.* A rank that hangs before its first batch is stored is `starting`, with no severity, for as long as its heartbeats go on or if it never sent one, so it is never a stall and never fires. (If its heartbeats stop it is `unresponsive`, which has a severity.) The first try of the real-server test showed it: the job stopped after 40 steps with the default `every=50`, so no step had left the device, and 58 s later the server still read `starting` with a floor of 2 s. So a job that hangs in its first `every` steps, or before its first step, with its process alive, is not caught by the watchdog today. That is the server's rule (`Progress::state`, and its test holds it at 1,500 s), and changing it is a Rust change and the author's call. *Changed the same day, at the author's word: "A rank that never stores a batch is no longer `starting` without limit".*
  - *Tested.* `tests/test_trigger.py` has 23 tests where it had 13. Eight are new under "A stall": a stall fires with no option at `critical` and not at `warning`, for each kind, through the watcher on a file that has stopped growing; a named stall does not fire again at `critical`, for each kind; only a stopped rank makes a stall critical; a change of kind or step fires again; a status that is no longer written; a failing command and an unreachable webhook are retried as for any incident. - [x] **A rank that never stores a batch is no longer `starting` without limit (decided by the author and built Nov 16, 2026 in a side session; merged Nov 17, 2026).** Found while building "a stall at critical fires": the capture holds `every` steps (50) before it writes any, so a job that hung in its first steps, or before its first step, stayed `starting` for ever, with no severity, no stall and no trigger. Branch `claude/heuristic-hertz-ef0bda`, on `main` at `9db7b81`.
    - **The rule the author chose** (the work counter, with a time bound for a rank that shows no work; critical at the start limit plus the critical time, not three times the limit):
        1. Once a rank's heartbeats show its work counter rising while it says `train`, it has taken a training step, and until its first stored batch the counter is its progress. It is `silent` when the counter has stood still past its threshold: `--silent-seconds`, or `--silent-factor` times its own time per step, never under three heartbeat intervals. It is `critical` at the larger of `--critical-seconds` and three thresholds, counted from the counter's last movement. Defaults: warning 300 s after the last step, critical at 900 s.
        2. A rank that has shown no training step (compilation, a data load, its first collective, or no heartbeat at all) is `starting` for `--start-seconds` (new flag, default 1800) since its last sign of work (the stream's opening, a tick, a change of phase). Then it is `silent` at `warning`, and `critical` `--critical-seconds` later: 2700 s by default.
        3. A warning given before the first batch keeps its clock over a server restart: the restored rank is counted from its counter's last movement or its last sign of work, not from when it was first seen, so it does not come back `critical`. Found in review after the first commit, and fixed with a test that failed before. *(Changed Nov 17, 2026: a restored rank is now judged from the restart, with a fresh window; see Decisions: "The trigger and the watchdog: one firing for one stall ...".)*
        4. A first report already above zero counts as a step taken. A tick in another phase is work and not a step. A declared pause after the first steps is `paused` under `--pause-seconds`, as later in a run. Stopped heartbeats are `unresponsive`, as before.
    - **What a slow start gets.** Steps of 30 s or 60 s keep the counter moving, so a first batch that takes 25 or 50 minutes raises nothing. A step slower than the floor sets a long threshold from the time per step. A first step that takes 40 minutes gets a warning at 30 that the step ends; one that takes longer than 45 minutes is called critical, and `--start-seconds` is the option to raise.
    - **What moved.**
        - `server/src/watchdog.rs`: `Progress::state`, `threshold`, the severity clock (`quiet_seconds`, `critical_after`), and `heartbeat`, which now keeps `stepped` and the last eight times per step. `main.rs` and `ingest.rs` carry `--start-seconds`.
        - `watchdog.json`: a rank can be `silent` with `last_step: null`, so a run can be `stalled` or `degraded` before any batch. Each rank entry has `work`; the top has `start_seconds`; `restart` has `stepped` and `step_seconds`, both read as absent from an older file. No new state name. The incident's step is 0.
        - Python: `_progress_incidents` and `run_state` are unchanged. `_describe_stream` says "Job stalled before its first batch was stored" and "has had no step stored yet" where it would have printed "after step 0" and "since step None". `sdk/README.md` says the rule.
        - Not moved: the golden files (they hold no status), `server/src/detect.rs`, the run state (a stall before the first batch reads `unknown`, as any stall does), `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.
    - **Tests.**
        - Rust: `before_the_first_batch_a_rank_is_starting_not_stopped` is replaced by `before_its_first_step_a_rank_is_starting_for_the_start_limit_then_silent`, with eight new tests beside it. `cargo test`: 82 unit tests and 28 server tests passed, 1 ignored as before. `cargo fmt --check` and CI's `cargo clippy` line are clean. Eleven deliberate breakages of the rule each failed at least one unit test.

But `checked` after one record, when the rule needs more, lets an agent read "checked, no incidents" as healthy, which is what "What was checked" set out to stop. Added: each kind says whether its rule has enough history yet, such as records seen against records needed. The attention kinds get the same treatment: today "too few probes" puts them in `not_checked`, which reads `unavailable` and makes the run `degraded` until a module's fourth probe (`ATTN_MIN_HISTORY` 2 and `ATTN_SUSTAIN` 2; with `probe_every` 1,000, about the 4,000th step, to be checked). A probe that failed stays `unavailable`.
    - This moves `run_state` in Python, in Rust and in the server's `watchdog.json`, and changes `kind_checks` in all 64 golden files, which are regenerated on purpose.

To do before the release: time the 64-rank benchmark again, since the parser is also on the live path, where one acknowledgement already reached 620 ms ("Re-timed with twenty-five kinds"). The author's remark, not a build order: moving the attention maps out of `probe_json` (G1) would win back far more than this costs.

    - The watchdog already separates the cases. A rank in a declared evaluation or checkpoint phase is `paused` and raises nothing, and `critical` comes only after 900 seconds (`DEFAULT_CRITICAL`), or three of the rank's own silence thresholds if that is longer.
    - The costs are lopsided: a false wake is one agent run, since the trigger fires once per incident, and a missed hang is hours of idle multi-GPU time, which is what the product promises to catch.
    - The agent can tell them apart: the incident carries the stuck frame and how long it has not changed.
    - Not changed: `job_stalled` and `rank_silent` stay observations, so the run state and the golden files do not move for this.
    - Read plainly: any stall at `critical` fires, whatever its assessment (a suspected hang, a lost connection). Open to narrowing.
    - The README is to say plainly that a local run with no server cannot detect a hang at all.

The inner linear layer (`w_g`) gives the logits, and so the assignments the router asked for; the ones kept after the capacity limit are not seen. The first step of any run is `named_modules()` and one forward to confirm what is hooked.

A router that fails this is not counted, and the session warns once at the next step, naming the router and the two ways out (`Router(..., indices=...)`, or the inner module whose output is the logits). `sdk/tests/test_router_guard.py` (4 tests, a stand-in for nanoMoE's router; two of them fail without the guard); the whole suite passes with it (1,224 and 81). - [x] **Optimizer ownership as evidence for `stalled_weights` (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** Equal float64 norms do not prove unchanged weights, so the incident stays a warning (worded "float64 norm did not change"). Each weight check records `optimizer_ownership`: whether an optimizer that has stepped holds each trainable parameter. It is `unknown`, never "not held", when ownership cannot be established: the optimizer hooks are off (`auto_step=False`), or no optimizer that stepped holds any of the model's parameters (DeepSpeed ZeRO, Megatron and FSDP1 step copies or flattened parameters). It travels in `extra_json` through the server. It is attached to the stalled incident as `optimizer` (`known` with the parameters no optimizer holds, or `unknown` with the reason) and is never an alarm of its own, because a parameter may be left out on purpose.
- [x] **Learning rate across a resume (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** `detect_resume_lr_changes` reads every attempt and, at the first step of each resumed attempt, compares its learning rate with the earlier attempt's: at the same step when the earlier attempt recorded it, otherwise continuing the earlier attempt's recent slope. A difference is reported as `resume_lr_change`, an observation and not a verdict: some restarts reset warmup on purpose, and a record cannot show the intent, so the incident says whether the resumed value equals the run's first learning rate and the summary says it is expected when the schedule was changed deliberately. A restart of the numbering is left to `repeated_steps`. A resume that cannot be compared because no learning rate was recorded goes into a new optional `not_checked` list in `evidence.json` (still `schema_version: 1`; readers that ignore the key lose nothing).
- [x] **TLS for the ingest stream (decided Nov 14, 2026):** the server uses tonic's `tls-aws-lc` feature, not `tls-ring`. `object_store` already brings rustls with the aws-lc provider, and with both providers compiled in rustls cannot pick one: a `tls-ring` build panics at startup when given `--tls-cert`/`--tls-key` (reproduced on the build before the switch). The sender trusts the system's certificate authorities for `grpcs://`; `TENSORQUILL_TLS_CA` (or `tls_root_certs=` on `FlightSender` and `send_file`) names a PEM file for a private or self-signed one, read at `init` so a wrong path fails before training starts. Tested on loopback only, with a self-signed certificate.
- [x] **The sender across `fork` (decided Nov 14, 2026):** a forked child (`DataLoader` workers on Linux, `multiprocessing`) gets a copy of the sender without its thread. `FlightSender.close()` compares the process ID with the one that created it and does nothing in a child, instead of an `os.register_at_fork` handler, which would add process-wide state for the same effect. A review claimed forked workers deadlock on their first batch; two tests (forked `DataLoader` workers during streaming, and a forked child that closes its copy) show no deadlock on macOS, and CI runs both on Linux.
- [ ] **Price for the post-Gate 4 hosted tier**, after Step 26 measures storage and server cost per run.
- [x] **Viewer (decided Nov 14, 2026):** Rerun is temporary. TensorQuill builds its own Rust viewer app on Rune that reads `evidence.json` and the JSONL/Parquet data directly; the `.rrd` export stays as the interim viewer until the app reaches parity. This replaces the earlier rule that a custom view was built only after a ≥2× win in Gate 1. *Postponed Nov 16, 2026 ("Rune viewer postponed"): Rerun stays the viewer and the app is not built until outside users ask for views Rerun cannot give.*
- [x] **Framework adapters (decided Nov 14, 2026):** PyTorch stays the only capture engine; adapters are thin and exist only where a framework hides the optimizer, clipping or scaler loop. Order:
  1. A Hugging Face `TrainerCallback` (`sdk/src/tensorquill/huggingface.py`, built Nov 14, 2026 and tested on CPU; named `huggingface` so the module does not shadow the `transformers` package), accepted on a dense single-GPU CUDA run with `sdk/examples/trainer_check.py`, which passes in toy mode on CPU and is still to run on a GPU. It does not wait for the multi-GPU gate. It covers TRL and PEFT; the first TRL test is `SFTTrainer` with LoRA, then DPO and GRPO separately (GRPO can generate rollouts in a separate vLLM server, which trainer-side capture does not see).
  2. An Accelerate recipe for custom loops. Built Nov 14, 2026 as `tensorquill.accelerator.watch_accelerator(session)` (`sdk/src/tensorquill/accelerator.py`), which the `Trainer` callback now uses too; tested on CPU. The loss is logged on every `backward` as the running sum for the step, because wrapping `AcceleratedOptimizer.step` does not work: a learning-rate scheduler replaces `step` on the optimizer instance.
  3. In parallel, validation of what is already supported: DDP over NCCL, then FSDP2 and tensor parallelism, Megatron-LM's `auto_step=False` recipe, and Lightning with `torch.compile`.
  4. One PPO example and one LeRobot imitation-learning example, to find what RL and robotics runs need beyond model internals (rollout and update boundaries, a link to policy evaluation).
  5. DeepSpeed: no adapter until someone brings a workload. Checked Nov 14, 2026 on a DeepSpeed 0.20.1 engine on CPU: at ZeRO stage 0 the default works; at stages 1 and 2 the optimizer steps flattened copies of the weights and automatic counting recorded nothing, silently. TensorQuill now warns after 10 optimizer steps with none on the model's parameters, and `RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md` has an `auto_step=False` recipe that records steps, the loss and DeepSpeed's global gradient norm. Stage 3, mixed precision and several ranks are not run. JAX, TensorFlow, tree models and FSDP1 stay out of scope.

  Checked against the Transformers (5.24.0.dev0) and Accelerate (1.16.0.dev0) sources, which shape the callback:
  - `Trainer` wraps, prepares and (through Accelerate's `TorchDynamoPlugin`) compiles the model before `on_train_begin`. Callbacks receive the unwrapped `trainer.model`. The callback must attach hooks before the first compiled forward, and a second `train()` on the same trainer is an open case.
  - Step boundaries are `on_pre_optimizer_step` and `on_optimizer_step`, which surround `optimizer.step()`; TensorQuill's own optimizer hooks already fire there. `on_log` fires every `logging_steps` with interval averages, after the step has ended; the callback does not record them in v1.
  - `accelerator.clip_grad_norm_` no longer calls `torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_` (it uses `get_total_norm` and `clip_grads_with_norm_`), so the clip wrapper records nothing under `Trainer` or Accelerate. This is new in 1.16.0.dev0; Accelerate 1.15.0 still calls it. The callback wraps `Accelerator.clip_grad_norm_` (and `Accelerator.backward`, for the per-step loss, which `Trainer` gives callbacks no access to) while a session is open, and skips recording when the inner PyTorch call already did.
  - `AcceleratedOptimizer.step` calls `scaler.step` and `scaler.update` itself, so `scaler=accelerator.scaler` should work unchanged.
  - Ray actors that share no process group all resolve to rank 0 and, given the same `run_id` and folder, append to one file. v1 answer: one learner writes, or each process passes its own `sink=` (documented in `RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md`). Per-role streams wait until actors are instrumented.
- [x] **CI (decided Nov 14, 2026):** GitHub Actions runs the CPU matrix on every push to `main` and every pull request, installing CPU-only PyTorch wheels. The GPU compiler gate is a manual `workflow_dispatch` job on a self-hosted runner: GitHub's free runners have no GPU, and a job that pull requests cannot start keeps outside code off that runner. On CPU the gate script runs on `aot_eager`, so Inductor and CUDA graphs are checked only by the `gpu` job.
- [x] **Default capture level (decided Nov 13, 2026):** `capture="routers"` is the always-on default for MoE models, and `capture="full"` is an opt-in debugging mode.
- [x] **Multi-GPU reporting (decided Nov 14, 2026):** every rank writes its own `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.jsonl` and computes `replica_spread` on `replica_group` every `replica_every=50` steps where `(step + 1) % replica_every == 0`.
- [x] **Ingest architecture and v1 server hosting (decided Nov 14, 2026):** the SDK always writes the local `.jsonl` file first; when `endpoint=` / `TENSORQUILL_ENDPOINT` is set, an opt-in sender tails that file from `<run_id>.rank<rank>.offset` and streams over Arrow Flight `DoPut` to the open-source, self-hosted Rust `tensorquill-server` binary (`server/`), which deduplicates by `(run_id, rank, attempt, step)` and writes Zstd Parquet parts via `object_store`.
- [x] **Ingest details the specification left open (decided Nov 14, 2026, with the build of `flight.py` and `server/`):**
  - *One long-lived `DoPut` stream per rank, acknowledged only when stored.* The sender keeps one stream open and the server answers with `PutResult.app_metadata = {"end_byte_offset": N}` (JSON, like the request metadata, so fields can be added) only after the Parquet part holding those rows is written. A stream per batch would either write a part every 50 steps instead of every 500, or acknowledge rows that exist only in server memory, and then a killed server would lose steps the sender had already moved past. The cost is that `.offset` trails the file by up to 10 seconds or 500 steps; the end-to-end test kills the server while it holds 100 unstored steps and loses none.
  - *Batches are at most 50 steps and 4 MiB even when catching up after an outage*, so the sender's memory stays bounded; a single line over 4 MiB is sent alone. gRPC keepalive (30 s) detects a dead connection, because a long-lived stream cannot carry a deadline.
  - *Three columns beyond the listed schema:* `step_skipped: Boolean` and `grad_scale: Float64` (written by `scaler=`, added after the schema was drafted), and `extra_json: Utf8`, which carries any key the schema does not know and any value that does not fit its column's type, so nothing is dropped and `row_to_record` returns the original dict. The `moe` field is the `moe_counts` column. Non-finite numbers are real NaN and infinity in the float columns and return to the strings `"nan"`, `"inf"`, `"-inf"` when read back.
  - *The server checks only the key columns* (`schema_version`, `run_id`, `rank`, `step`, and `attempt` when present) and the batch metadata, and writes whatever other columns arrive. A column added by a newer SDK then needs no server release; a schema change in mid-buffer starts a new part, because a part has one schema.
  - *The watermark is read when an attempt's first batch arrives*, from the footer of its newest part (by the last step in the file name; a part with a foreign name is always opened), not for every run at startup, which would list the whole store.
  - *Only the footer is fetched (decided Nov 14, 2026).* One suffix range request of 64 KiB, a bounded range on stores that refuse suffix requests, and a second read only for a larger footer; page indexes are skipped, since the row-group statistics hold the step range. The server's own parts measured 7.2–7.7 KiB of footer on router-only and full captures, so 8 KiB, the first choice, left about 500 bytes; DataFusion's default for the same reader call is 512 KiB, which also covers page indexes. Before this, each part opened was downloaded in full.
  - *Quota.* Buffered bytes are the rows' own Arrow bytes (`get_slice_memory_size`): `get_array_memory_size` counts a decoded message's shared buffer once per column. A batch that does not fit first writes that rank's own buffer; if it still does not fit, the reply is `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`. A batch larger than the whole quota is accepted when nothing else is buffered, so it cannot wait forever. Messages up to 64 MiB are decoded.
  - *Setup errors raise at `init`; failures while running never do.* A bad endpoint scheme, a missing `pyarrow`, or `endpoint=` with a sink that has no file raises before training starts. After that the sender only records `last_error`, backs off and retries, and `close()` prints the local path if bytes are unconfirmed. `tensorquill.flight.send_file` sends what a finished run still owes.
  - *Complete lines that are not step records of this run are skipped and counted* (`skipped_lines`), which is what a killed writer leaves when the next process appends after its half line.
  - *`run_id` on the server is limited to letters, digits, `-`, `_` and `.`*, because it becomes a directory name; other IDs are refused with `INVALID_ARGUMENT`.
  - *On `SIGTERM` the server does not wait for open streams*, which never end on their own: it refuses new batches, writes every buffer and exits.
  - *Consequence of watermark deduplication, handled Nov 14, 2026 by attempts (see above):* a run restarted from an earlier step under the same `run_id` used to have its repeated step numbers dropped by the server.
- [x] **Where detectors run (decided Nov 14, 2026):** detectors run in Python (`sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py`) on local `.jsonl` or live server Parquet (`python -m tensorquill --watch`), and will only be ported into the Rust server if the 64-rank benchmark exceeds 250 ms per 50-step flush or 1 CPU core. *Benchmarked Nov 15, 2026: at Granite's shape the Python detectors exceed both limits, so the condition is met, and the port began the same day (see the entry on live detection in Rust).*
- [x] **Who explains failures (decided Nov 14, 2026):** v1 hands `evidence.json` and Rerun screenshots to the team's own coding agent; a built-in VLM explainer is postponed.
- [x] **Tier 3 activation replay and MLflow scalar importer (decided Nov 14, 2026):** both postponed from v1 (revisit the MLflow importer only if ≥3 engineers join).
- [x] **Data-wait tests hold what is true by construction (decided Nov 17, 2026).** `test_with_loader_workers` failed once in CI (the second batch's wait was 8 ms against a 10 ms floor) and 8 of 8 times on a Mac (Python 3.13, torch 2.15, spawn). The timer was right. A `DataLoader` worker fetches the next batch while the loop runs the step before, so when the first step is slow the batch is already waiting and the loop really does not wait for it. The test now holds three things: each wait is above zero and no longer than the test's own stopwatch around that `next()`; and since one worker hands over batch `k` no sooner than `k` fetches after the loader starts, the waits add up to at least the fetch time minus the time the loop itself was busy. The tests for counting twice and for other threads and the eval phase now also hold each wait to the test's own stopwatch, not a fixed number of milliseconds. A timer reading zero, counting twice, or ignoring the thread, phase or `_fetching` checks each fails a test. Open: the upper bound in `_assert_fetch_timed` (fetch time below `FETCH + MOVE/2` per batch, used by the watch, `time_data`, Lightning, Accelerate and Hugging Face tests) still depends on how long a sleep overshoots on a loaded runner. A replacement, `step_time − data_wait ≥ MOVE` on each step after the first, held on the watch and Lightning runs, but it was not tried on Accelerate or Hugging Face, which do not run locally.
- [x] **The data-wait move check no longer depends on runner speed (decided Nov 17, 2026).** This closes the open item of the entry above. `_assert_fetch_timed` in `sdk/tests/test_datawait.py` (used by the watch, `time_data`, Accelerate, Lightning and Hugging Face Trainer tests) no longer caps the waits at `FETCH + MOVE/2` per batch, which a loaded runner's late sleeps could overshoot. It now requires, on every step after the first, `step_time − data_wait ≥ moves × MOVE`: each step sleeps that long after the loader hands the batch over, so that much is outside `next()` however slow the machine, and a load only adds to it. The fetch floor and `step_time ≥ data_wait` stay. The Hugging Face test's steps hold two microbatches (`moves=2`), and the plain-iterable half of the `time_data` test now sleeps `MOVE` per batch too. Checked from outside the product with a pytest plugin: a timer that also counts the move, or the whole step, fails all five tests inside the helper (the `time_data` one first at its own stopwatch check, and inside the helper when only its second half is faulty). The file passed 50 of 50 runs with no added load and 50 of 50 beside 8 CPU-spinning processes on an 8-core Mac (Python 3.12, torch 2.15, accelerate 1.16, transformers 5.23, lightning 2.6.6), none skipped; other sessions' tests shared the machine during the unloaded runs. Limits: the first record has no `step_time` and is not checked this way; on a loaded machine a faulty timer can slip past on one step that has a `MOVE` or more of other time (a Lightning step that runs validation), but not on all of them. The other timing checks in the file are floors, the test's own stopwatch, or a 10 s hang guard, and only get looser under load. Not run on Linux.
- [x] **The thresholds line is printed for a local file (built Nov 17, 2026 in a side session).** Closes the open item of Nov 17, 2026 "the thresholds line is not printed for a local file" (item 6 of "A stall at critical fires the trigger").
  - *What was missing.* Before this, no output of `python -m tensorquill` on a local `.jsonl` file printed the line when a threshold flag was off the default: not one read, `--rank 0`, `--compact`, or `--watch`, and not with the Python detectors or with `--detectors rust`. A read of a store's parts without `--watch` did not print it either. All of these go through `build_evidence`, whose evidence has no `detection` key and kept three of the five options as keys of their own, without `drift_tolerance` or the `top_k` that was given. `--json` does not exist, and `--rank all` refuses a file.
  - *What was built.* When one of the five options is not the default, `build_evidence` lists all five it ran with as `thresholds`. That is the name the server's report gives them, and the key has the shape of `detection.thresholds`, with the values that were asked for. The Rust path already refuses a binary that applied other values. `thresholds_line` reads `detection.thresholds` when the evidence has a `detection` key, and otherwise this key, as `the detectors ran here with ...`. No command-line code changed: every output already prints `incident_summary`.
  - *At the defaults nothing changed.* Evidence at the defaults, including evidence with an option typed at its default value, has no key and prints no line. The Gate 1 and bake-off outputs (`harness.py build` and `generate.py`, 73 `.json` and `.txt` files) were made before and after this change and are byte-identical. The golden files freeze other keys and pass.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_thresholds_line.py` now has 20 tests (it had 12). The new tests cover the evidence key for each option and for all five, and `top_k` per router. They also cover one read, `--rank 0` and `--compact`, each with flags off the defaults (the line once, before the run state) and at them (none typed, and each typed at its default value: the same stdout and the same evidence bytes). They cover `--watch` and `--watch --compact`, the Rust detectors in one read, `--rank 0`, `--compact` and `--watch` (the same line and key as the Python ones; not `--watch --compact`), and a read of a store's parts without `--watch`. Without pyarrow, 15 pass and 5 skip; the 5 need pyarrow and the server binary, and the file is already in CI's server job. One assertion was changed on purpose: the old one held that a local file with options off the defaults gives no line.
  - *Run, macOS, this worktree's release binary.* Python, the whole suite in `sdk/`: 1,360 passed. The 81 experiment tests passed.
  - *Open.*
    - Evidence at the defaults does not list its thresholds. Its file says the defaults applied only by having no key, which is also what a file from before the key looks like.
    - A read of a store without `--watch`, and `--watch --recompute`, run the detectors here with the defaults or with the flags typed. They do not take the thresholds the server applied, as `--watch` does. Their evidence has no `detection` key, so the line comes from the new key: it is right about what ran here, but the read can disagree with the server's report.
    - Evidence judged here with a server's non-default thresholds now also has the top-level key. This covers a watcher whose report cannot be used (`missing`, `stale`, `other_thresholds`), which also has `detection.thresholds`; the line reads `detection` first and prints once. It also covers `compare` on a rank whose report cannot be used (`compare.py` passes the applied thresholds to `build_evidence`). The `compare` tests pass, but `compare` was not run against such a store.
  - *Not run:* Linux and CI; `cargo test` (no Rust file changed).
- [x] **The trigger and the watchdog: one firing for one stall, a lost connection, compact evidence, a fresh window after a restart (decided by the author Nov 17, 2026; built the same day in a side session).** Branch `claude/trigger-watchdog-706634`, on `main` at `19b82ec`. In `sdk/src/tensorquill/trigger.py`, `detect_watchdog_incidents` and the stall text in `evidence.py`, and `server/src/watchdog.rs`. Reference repos read first: `wandb`'s `run.alert` holds an alert back by its title within a run, not by the step (taken: key a stall by its run); `torch.distributed.elastic` fails a worker group as a unit when one worker dies and reports a dead process by its exit signal, apart from a hang, and drops a rendezvous node whose keep-alive has expired (taken: the lost rank labels a job that stops together); `mlflow`'s webhooks carry a delivery id (already used); `lightning` only requeues on a SLURM signal, nothing on a restart window.
    1. **One firing for one stall.** The trigger keys `job_stalled` and `rank_silent` by the run, not by rank, kind and step; other kinds keep their key. A silent rank that becomes a stalled job, and a second rank that falls silent at an earlier step, fire nothing more. A `*.fired.json` of the earlier version loads unchanged (`STATE_VERSION` 1): any stall it lists is the run's stall, so an upgrade fires nothing again. A stall named with `--fire-kind` that fired at `warning` does not fire at `critical`, nor does the `job_stalled` a named `rank_silent` becomes. A try still to make for a stall sends the stall as it reads now, under the firing's own id. *Open:* a run that stalls, fires, steps again and stalls a second time fires nothing for the second stall under this rule; not decided. Also open: a state file of the earlier version that holds two stall firings with tries still to make sends both, each as the current stall. A mixed `job_stalled` read as `lost_connection` is no longer upgraded to `confirmed_hang` by a Flight Recorder dump (the stuck collective is then the survivors waiting). An `unresponsive` rank's verdict held over a restart is forgotten if the server restarts again inside the window.
    2. **A killed job fires, labelled as a lost connection.** No new kind (still 32; golden files and Gate 1 fixtures unchanged). The label is the stall's existing `assessment`, with a new `lost_ranks` field and summary text: `lost_connection` reads "The connection of rank N dropped and did not come back: read as a lost connection, not a hang", names an out-of-memory kill as the likely cause, and drops the sentence that a hang and a killed job look the same. *The mix, decided here:* a stall reads as a lost connection when a lost rank explains it: every stopped rank is lost, or the whole job stopped (`job_stalled`) and at least one rank is lost, since connected ranks of a job that stops together wait for the one that is gone (an OOM-killed rank and the others blocked in a collective was a `suspected_hang` before). A lost rank beside a silent one while other ranks step stays `suspected_hang`, with the lost rank in `lost_ranks`. Python and Rust build it alike (a Rust test and a Python test on the same cases; a real-server test compares `status["incidents"]` with `detect_watchdog_incidents(status)`). The command gets `TENSORQUILL_INCIDENT_ASSESSMENT`, the JSON document a top-level `assessment` (null for other incidents; payload version still 1), and the trigger's log line says `(lost connection)`.
    3. **The trigger turns on `--compact`.** On the command line, `--on-incident` or `--webhook` with no `--compact` keeps writing the full evidence as before, and the trigger writes `<name>.compact.json` beside it on every look and hands that file over: `TENSORQUILL_EVIDENCE` and the document's `evidence` name the compact file, because the agent that is woken should read the small one and the full file must stay for everything else. Built in `trigger.py` (`Trigger(compact_window=...)`, set by `from_args`), so the watcher's own writes are untouched. With `--compact` given, the watcher writes the compact file alone, as before, and that is handed over. A compact file that cannot be written is said once and the full file is handed over. The state file's name is unchanged. Checked with `--rank all --watch --webhook` against a real server and a local listener.
    4. **A fresh window after a server restart.** This changes the logged rule "a warning given before the first batch keeps its clock over a server restart" (the Nov 16 entry "A rank that never stores a batch ...", item 3) and the Nov 15 rule that a restored stopped rank keeps its quiet time: now every restored rank that was not finished is judged from the restart. A rank that has shown no training step gets `--start-seconds`; one that has stepped gets its own silence threshold (`--silent-seconds`, or its slower cadence), chosen over the start window because its process went on through the restart and has only to reconnect (the sender's backoff is capped at 30 s) and send its next batch, which the threshold already allows for, while the start window is sized for compilation and data loading it does not do again. Built as a restart instant that no verdict clock starts before; the clocks the status shows (`seconds_since_progress`, `phase_seconds`, pauses) are kept. Inside the window a rank whose sender has not come back is `reconnecting`; after it `lost`, or `unresponsive` if its heartbeats had already stopped (a frozen process cannot reconnect, and is not a lost connection), and a rank that never stepped is critical `--critical-seconds` after its window. *The cost, with the defaults (pinned by a Rust test on a made clock with `WatchdogConfig::default()`):* a hang or a killed job that spans a restart is called later by the time it had already been quiet: a rank hung or killed 800 s before the restart (a warning) is critical 900 s after it instead of 100 s, 800 s later; a rank that never stepped is critical 2,700 s after the restart. A stall that was already critical (and fired) reads healthy for the window, and a server that restarts more often than the window never calls a hang.
    - *Verified* on macOS with Python 3.12 and this branch's release server: `cargo test` 88 unit and 31 integration tests pass (1 ignored, as before), clippy with `-D warnings` and `cargo fmt --check` clean; the whole Python suite with the experiments' 81 tests: 1,438 passed and 1 failed (`test_object_store_e2e.py`'s restart test, which pinned the old restart rule; rewritten, that file passed alone, 3 tests); on the final tree (`54d7173`) the whole run again: 1,439 passed, 0 failed; with `pyarrow` hidden and no server binary, the four changed test files pass or skip (46 passed, 13 skipped). Tests changed because they pinned the rules replaced here: in `test_trigger.py` the stall that fired again on a change of kind or step, in `test_watchdog.py` a mixed stall read as a suspected hang, and every restart test (`watchdog.rs`, `server/tests/ingest_test.rs`, `test_object_store_e2e.py`). New: in `test_trigger.py` (28 tests, 5 new) the run key, an old state file, the compact hand-over, and three real-server tests: a silent rank that becomes a stalled job posts once to a `--rank all --watch --webhook` listener (compact file named, full file kept); a rank killed with SIGKILL beside a live one reads as a lost connection and starts the command once, Rust's and Python's incidents equal; a hung rank at `warning` across a SIGKILL restart of the server fires nothing for 9 s and once at critical, 12 s after the restart. The last two fail against the server binary built before this change. In `watchdog.rs`, four new tests (the mix rule, the fresh window before a first batch, an unresponsive rank after its window, the cost at the default timings). Golden files and the Gate 1 fixtures are unchanged.
    - *Not verified:* the default timings (300, 900, 1,800 s) have never been run for real; the real-server tests use 2 s and 6 s, 3 s and 12 s. Linux and CI (macOS only). A real out-of-memory kill (SIGKILL from the test stands in for it), a real stuck collective, a real agent, an outside webhook. *For the merge:* no new test file; the three new real-server tests are in `tests/test_trigger.py`, already in CI's server job.
